).

We can now finally prove Proposition
A.3
.

Proof of Proposition
A.3
.

Suppose
\Psi:\thinspace\mathcal{A}_{\Lambda^{\prime}}\to\mathcal{A}_{\Lambda^{\prime}}
is a chain map and an isomorphism. By Lemma
A.6
, we have
u_{1},\ldots,u_{2s}
so that (

∗

‣
A.2
) holds for all Reeb chords of
\Lambda^{\prime}
. Now the strands
1,\ldots,2s
are joined in pairs at the left end of
\Lambda^{\prime}
, and joined in pairs again at the right end. On the left end, for
k=1,\ldots,s
, strands
2k-1
and
2k
are connected, and this yields an embedded disk with a single corner at
a_{2k-1,2k}^{1,1}
, which must be a
+
corner by Stokes. This contributes a constant (
1
) term to
\delta(a_{2k-1,2k}^{1,1})
. Since
\Psi
is a chain map and
\Psi(a_{2k-1,2k}^{1,1})=u_{2k}u_{2k-1}^{-1}a_{2k-1,2k}^{1,1}
by (

∗

‣
A.2
), it follows that
u_{2k-1}=u_{2k}
.

More generally, the same argument shows that if strands
i
and
j
are joined at either end of
\Lambda^{\prime}
, then
u_{i}=u_{j}
. It follows that
u_{i}=u_{j}
whenever
i
and
j
are part of the same connected component of
\Lambda^{\prime}
. Thus we may remove duplicates and rename
u_{1},\ldots,u_{2s}
as
u_{1},\ldots,u_{m}
, where
m
is the number of components of
\Lambda^{\prime}
. Then (

∗

‣
A.2
) becomes precisely the condition for
\Psi
to be a link automorphism of
\Lambda^{\prime}
, and we are done.
∎

A.3.
Proof of Proposition
A.1

With the auxiliary result Proposition
A.3
in hand, we next prove Proposition
A.1
. Suppose that
\Lambda_{+}
and
\Lambda_{-}
are related by a saddle cobordism at a contractible crossing flanked by mini-dips, as in the statement of Proposition
A.3
or the right hand side of Figure
37
.
We first show that the desired map
\Phi
is indeed a chain map, and then proceed to the main proof.

Lemma A.7
.

The map
\Phi:\thinspace\mathcal{A}_{\Lambda_{+}}\to\mathcal{A}_{\Lambda_{-}}
defined in Proposition
A.1
is a chain map:
\Phi\circ\partial_{+}=\partial_{-}\circ\Phi
.

Proof.

We show that
\Phi\circ\partial_{+}
and
\partial_{-}\circ\Phi
agree on all Reeb chords of
\Lambda_{+}
. Note that
\partial_{+}(a)=0
, so
\Phi(\partial_{+}(a))=0=\partial_{-}(s)=\partial_{-}(\Phi(a))
. Also if we denote the mini-dip crossing next to
a_{1}
by
a_{3}
, then
\partial_{+}(a_{1})=\partial_{-}(a_{1})=-a_{3}
, so
\Phi(\partial_{+}(a_{1}))=-\Phi(a_{3})=-a_{3}=\partial_{-}(a_{1}-s^{-1})=\partial_{-}(\Phi(a_{1}))
; similarly
\Phi(\partial_{+}(a_{2}))=\partial_{-}(\Phi(a_{2}))
.

Figure 45.
Labeling the strands of
\Lambda_{+}
(left) and
\Lambda_{-}
(right) in the cobordism region, and disks that pass through the cobordism region and contribute to
\partial_{+}(a_{i})
and
\partial_{-}(a_{i})
.

Now suppose that
a_{i}
is a Reeb chord of
\Lambda_{+}
besides
a,a_{1},a_{2}
: we need to show that
\Phi(\partial_{+}(a_{i}))=\partial_{-}(a_{i})
. The disks that make up the differentials
\partial_{+}(a_{i})
and
\partial_{-}(a_{i})
are exactly the same except where they pass through the cobordism region encompassing
a_{1},a,a_{2}
. Where these disks pass through the cobordism region, there is also a precise correspondence between the disks for
\partial_{+}(a_{i})
and
\partial_{-}(a_{i})
. The oriented boundary of such a disk enters the region on one of the strands on the left and exits on one of the strands on the right, or it enters on the right and exits on the left. If we label the strands as shown in Figure
45
, then for instance any disk contributing to
\partial_{-}(a_{i})
that enters on the left on strand 1 and exits on the right on strand 2 must pass
s
and turn a corner at
a_{1}
; there are two corresponding disks contributing to
\partial_{+}(a_{i})
with the same enter and exit data, one of which turns no corners in the cobordism region and one of which turns corners at
a
and
a_{1}
. See Figure
45
; the result replaces a monomial
sa_{1}
in
\partial_{-}(a_{i})
by
1+aa_{1}
in
\partial_{+}(a_{i})
. In all, there are
8
ways to pass through the cobordism region, with resulting contributions to
\partial_{\pm}(a_{i})
as follows:

\begin{array}[]{|c|c|c||c|c|c|}\hline\cr&\partial_{+}(a_{i})&\partial_{-}(a_{i})&&\partial_{+}(a_{i})&\partial_{-}(a_{i})\\
\hline\cr\hline\cr 1\rightarrow 1&a&s&1\leftarrow 1&-a_{1}-a_{2}-a_{1}aa_{2}&s^{-1}-a_{1}sa_{2}\\
\hline\cr 1\rightarrow 2&1+aa_{1}&sa_{1}&1\leftarrow 2&1+aa_{2}&sa_{2}\\
\hline\cr 2\rightarrow 1&1+a_{2}a&a_{2}s&2\leftarrow 1&1+a_{1}a&a_{1}s\\
\hline\cr 2\rightarrow 2&a_{1}+a_{2}+a_{2}aa_{1}&-s^{-1}+a_{2}sa_{1}&2\leftarrow 2&-a&-s\\
\hline\cr\end{array}

Now an inspection of this table shows that each entry in the
\partial_{-}(a_{i})
column is obtained from the corresponding entry in the
\partial_{+}(a_{i})
column by replacing
a,a_{1},a_{2}
by
s,a_{1}-s^{-1},a_{2}-s^{-1}
respectively. It immediately follows that
\Phi(\partial_{+}(a_{i}))=\partial_{-}(a_{i})
.
∎

We now have a chain map
\Phi:\thinspace\mathcal{A}_{\Lambda_{+}}\to\mathcal{A}_{\Lambda_{-}}
. In order to prove Proposition
A.1
, we want to show that this is equal to the geometric cobordism map
\Phi_{L_{a}}
up to a link automorphism. To do this, we will first localize the differentials of
\Lambda_{\pm}
by introducing splashes in the spirit of Section
A.2
. In what follows, we continue to refer to the small region of
\Lambda_{\pm}
containing
a_{1}
and
a_{2}
(and
a
for
\Lambda_{+}
) as the “cobordism region”, outside of which
\Lambda_{+}
and
\Lambda_{-}
coincide. We now change
\Lambda_{-}
by a sequence of Reidemeister II moves that avoid the cobordism region, first pulling all vertical tangencies of
\Pi_{xy}(\Lambda_{-})
left or right so that they line up vertically, then adding splashes to separate any crossings in
\Pi_{xy}(\Lambda_{-})
or
\Pi_{xz}(\Lambda_{-})
outside the cobordism region. From this we obtain a link
\Lambda_{-}^{\prime}
, Legendrian isotopic to
\Lambda_{-}
, for which there are
x_{0}<x_{1}<\cdots<x_{p}
such that:

•

all vertical tangencies lie on
x=x_{0}
or
x=x_{p}
, and the number of vertical tangencies on each of these lines is at least
2
;

•

there is a collection of splashes in a neighborhood of
x=x_{i}
for
i=1,\ldots,p-1
;

•

there is one
i\in\{1,\ldots,p-2\}
such that
[x_{i},x_{i+1}]
contains the cobordism region, and in that interval
[x_{i},x_{i+1}]
the only crossings in either
\Pi_{xy}(\Lambda_{-})
or
\Pi_{xz}(\Lambda_{-})
are between the two strands involved in the cobordism region;

•

for every other
i=1,\ldots,p-2
, in the interval
[x_{i},x_{i+1}]
there is exactly one crossing in either
\Pi_{xy}(\Lambda_{-})
or
\Pi_{xz}(\Lambda_{-})
, and no crossing in the other;

•

[x_{0},x_{1}]
and
[x_{p-1},x_{p}]
contain no crossings in
\Pi_{xy}(\Lambda_{-})
or
\Pi_{xz}(\Lambda_{-})
.

In short, we follow the prescription from Section
A.2
, except that we do not separate the crossings in the cobordism region from each other.

If we follow the same sequence of Reidemeister II moves going from
\Lambda_{-}
to
\Lambda_{-}^{\prime}
, but start with
\Lambda_{+}
, then we obtain a Legendrian link
\Lambda_{+}^{\prime}
that differs from
\Lambda_{-}^{\prime}
only in the cobordism region. We summarize the picture as follows:

\textstyle{\Lambda_{+}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\Lambda_{+}^{\prime}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\Lambda_{-}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\Lambda_{-}^{\prime},}

where the horizontal arrows are Legendrian isotopies given by (the same) Reidemeister II moves, and the vertical arrows are (identical) elementary saddle cobordisms. Note that the saddle cobordism between
\Lambda_{+}
and
\Lambda_{-}
is Hamiltonian isotopic to the concatenation of the three cobordisms specified by the other three sides of the square: from top to bottom, the isotopy from
\Lambda_{+}
to
\Lambda_{+}^{\prime}
, followed by the saddle cobordism between
\Lambda_{+}^{\prime}
and
\Lambda_{-}^{\prime}
, followed by the isotopy from
\Lambda_{-}^{\prime}
to
\Lambda_{-}
. By
[
EHK16
,
Kar20
]
, the cobordism map
\Phi_{L_{a}}:\thinspace\mathcal{A}_{\Lambda_{+}}\to\mathcal{A}_{\Lambda_{-}}
is chain homotopy equivalent to the composition of the cobordism maps given by the three cobordisms. We will show that this composition is the map
\Phi
from the statement of Proposition
A.1
.

We first consider the cobordism map
\Phi^{\prime}:\mathcal{A}_{\Lambda_{+}^{\prime}}\to\mathcal{A}_{\Lambda_{-}^{\prime}}
. By Lemma
A.7
, we know of another chain map
\Phi_{1}:\thinspace\mathcal{A}_{\Lambda_{+}^{\prime}}\to\mathcal{A}_{\Lambda_{-}^{\prime}}
: this is defined by
\Phi_{1}(a)=s
,
\Phi_{1}(a_{1})=a_{1}-s^{-1}
,
\Phi_{1}(a_{2})=a_{2}-s^{-1}
, and
\Phi_{1}(a_{i})=a_{i}
for all other Reeb chords
a_{i}
. Since
[
EHK16
]
gives a formula for geometric cobordism maps mod
2
and this formula is especially simple in our case, we know that the geometric map
\Phi^{\prime}
agrees with
\Phi_{1}
up to signs. By replacing
s
by
-s
if necessary, we can assume that
\Phi^{\prime}(a)=s
.

Lemma A.8
.

There is a link automorphism
\Omega:\thinspace\mathcal{A}_{\Lambda_{-}^{\prime}}\to\mathcal{A}_{\Lambda_{-}^{\prime}}
such that
\Phi^{\prime}=\Omega\circ\Phi_{1}
.

Proof.

Write
\partial_{+}^{\prime}
and
\partial_{-}^{\prime}
for the differentials on
\mathcal{A}_{\Lambda_{+}^{\prime}}
and
\mathcal{A}_{\Lambda_{-}^{\prime}}
respectively.

Since the terms in
\Phi^{\prime}
agree with the terms in
\Phi_{1}
up to sign, there are signs
\sigma_{i}\in\{\pm 1\}
such that
\Phi^{\prime}(a_{1})=\sigma_{1}a_{1}\pm s^{-1}
,
\Phi^{\prime}(a_{2})=\sigma_{2}a_{2}\pm s^{-1}
, and
\Phi^{\prime}(a_{i})=\sigma_{i}a_{i}
for all other
i
. In fact, because
\Phi^{\prime}
is a chain map, we must more specifically have
\Phi^{\prime}(a_{1})=\sigma_{1}a_{1}-s^{-1}
and
\Phi^{\prime}(a_{2})=\sigma_{2}a_{2}-s^{-1}
. To see this for
a_{1}
(with a similar argument for
a_{2}
), we use the fact that
\Lambda_{\pm}^{\prime}
have more than
2
strands joining left and right in the
x
direction, as stipulated in their construction. In particular, there is a strand of
\Lambda_{\pm}^{\prime}
that lies either above or below the cobordism region in the
xy
projection. Assume this strand lies above (the argument for below is very similar). The splashes from this strand on either side of the cobordism region intersect the strands from the cobordism region in a number of crossings, two of which are labeled
a_{3}
and
a_{4}
in Figure
46
. In
\Pi_{xy}(\Lambda_{+}^{\prime})
, there is a standard bigon with corners at
a_{3}
and
a_{4}
, contributing either
a_{4}
to
\partial_{+}^{\prime}(a_{3})
or
a_{3}
to
\partial_{+}^{\prime}(a_{4})
. For definiteness assume the former (the argument is same for the latter). An inspection of Figure
46
shows that
\partial_{+}^{\prime}(a_{3})
contains the terms
\pm(1+a_{1}a)a_{4}
while
\partial_{-}^{\prime}(a_{3})
contains
\pm a_{1}sa_{4}
, and furthermore that these are the only terms in
\partial_{\pm}^{\prime}(a_{3})
that involve
a_{4}
. Since
\partial_{-}\Phi^{\prime}(a_{3})=\Phi^{\prime}\partial_{+}(a_{3})
, we must have
\pm a_{1}sa_{4}=\Phi^{\prime}((1+a_{1}a)a_{4})=\pm(1+(\sigma_{1}a_{1}\pm s^{-1})s)a_{4}
, which implies that the
\pm
sign is
-
as claimed.

Figure 46.
Splashes on either side of the cobordism region in
\Lambda_{+}^{\prime}
(left) and
\Lambda_{-}^{\prime}
(right), with relevant crossings labeled.

Now let
\Omega
to be the algebra automorphism of
\mathcal{A}_{\Lambda_{-}^{\prime}}
defined by
\Omega(a_{i})=\sigma_{i}a_{i}
for all
i
; then by our expression for
\Phi^{\prime}
, we have
\Phi^{\prime}=\Omega\circ\Phi_{1}
.
It follows from the fact that
\Phi^{\prime}
and
\Phi_{1}
are both chain maps that
\Omega
is also a chain map. Indeed, for any
i
we have

\partial_{-}^{\prime}\Omega a_{i}=\partial_{-}^{\prime}\Phi^{\prime}a_{i}=\Phi^{\prime}\partial_{+}^{\prime}a_{i}=\Omega\Phi_{1}\partial_{+}^{\prime}a_{i}=\Omega\partial_{-}^{\prime}\Phi_{1}a_{i}=\Omega\partial_{-}^{\prime}a_{i},

where when
i=1,2
the first and last equality follow from the fact that
\partial_{-}^{\prime}(s^{-1})=0
.

It remains to show that
\Omega
is a link automorphism of
\Lambda_{-}^{\prime}
. To do this, we use the fact that
\Omega
is a diagonal automorphism of
\mathcal{A}_{\Lambda_{-}^{\prime}}
and a chain map, and appeal to a variant of Proposition
A.3
. We cannot use Proposition
A.3
directly because
\Lambda_{-}^{\prime}
does not have a splash between
a_{1}
and
a_{2}
. However, we can still follow the inductive proof of Proposition
A.3
in this setting. The only thing we need to check is the inductive step where we are given that
a_{1}
satisfies the condition (

∗

‣
A.2
) from the proof and need to conclude that
a_{2}
also satisfies this condition. To do this, let
a_{3}
and
a_{5}
be the crossings depicted in Figure
46
, and note that there is a standard bigon in
\Pi_{xy}(\Lambda_{-}^{\prime})
with corners at
a_{3}
and
a_{5}
. If the positive corner of this bigon is at
a_{3}
, then
\partial_{-}^{\prime}(a_{3})
contains the terms
(a_{1}sa_{2}-s^{-1})a_{5}
, while if the positive corner is at
a_{5}
, then
\partial_{-}^{\prime}(a_{5})
contains the terms
a_{3}(a_{1}sa_{2}-s^{-1})
. In either case, since
\Omega
is a chain map,
\Omega(a_{1})s\Omega(a_{2})-s^{-1}
must be equal to
\pm(a_{1}sa_{2}-s^{-1})
. Since
a_{1}
satisfies(

∗

‣
A.2
),
\Omega(a_{1})=u_{r(a_{1})}u_{c(a_{1})}^{-1}a_{1}
; but this implies that

\Omega(a_{2})=(u_{r(a_{1})}u_{c(a_{1})}^{-1})^{-1}a_{2}=u_{r(a_{2})}u_{c(a_{2})}^{-1}a_{2}
and so
a_{2}
satisfies (

∗

‣
A.2
), as desired. This completes the proof of Lemma
A.8
.
∎

We next examine the maps given by the Legendrian isotopies between
\Lambda_{+}
and
\Lambda_{+}^{\prime}
, and between
\Lambda_{-}
and
\Lambda_{-}^{\prime}
.
Suppose that
\Lambda_{-}^{\prime}
is obtained from
\Lambda_{-}
by
N
Reidemeister II moves. Then we can follow
[
Che02
,
ENS02
]
to construct a DGA isomorphism
\Psi_{-}
between
\mathcal{A}_{\Lambda_{-}^{\prime}}
and the DGA
S^{N}(\mathcal{A}_{\Lambda_{-}})
given by stabilizing
\mathcal{A}_{\Lambda_{-}}

N
times (adding
2N
generators in the process). This isomorphism comes from
N
applications of the isomorphism coming from a single Reidemeister II move, as already described in Section
4.1
. By that construction, if we start with
\Lambda_{-}
and add the Reidemeister II moves one by one, we see that the nontrivial parts of
\Psi_{-}
come from disks with two positive punctures, one of which is at a crossing in the Reidemeister II move. By inspection, there is no point at which there is such a disk where the other positive puncture is at either
a_{1}
or
a_{2}
, and it follows that
\Psi_{-}(a_{1})=a_{1}
and
\Psi_{-}(a_{2})=a_{2}
.

Similarly, since
\Lambda_{+}^{\prime}
is obtained from
\Lambda_{+}
by the same Reidemeister II moves, we have a DGA isomorphism
\Psi_{+}
between
\mathcal{A}_{\Lambda_{+}^{\prime}}
and
S^{N}(\mathcal{A}_{\Lambda_{+}})
, and
\Psi_{+}(a_{1})=a_{1}
,
\Psi_{+}(a)=a
,
\Psi_{+}(a_{2})=a_{2}
. Indeed, we can say more about the relation between
\Psi_{+}
and
\Psi_{-}
. The key point is that there is a precise correspondence between the twice-positive-punctured disks that determine
\Psi_{+}
and the twice-positive-punctured disks that determine
\Psi_{-}
: algebraically, one obtains the latter from the former by replacing
a,a_{1},a_{2}
by
s,a_{1}-s^{-1},a_{2}-s^{-1}
just as in the proof of Lemma
A.7
. Consequently, for any Reeb chord
a_{i}
of
\Lambda_{+}^{\prime}
(and thus of
\Lambda_{-}^{\prime}
) besides
a,a_{1},a_{2}
,
\Psi_{-}(a_{i})
is obtained from
\Psi_{+}(a_{i})
by this algebraic replacement.

Put another way, let
\Phi_{1}
be as above, and similarly define
\Phi_{2}:\thinspace S^{N}(\mathcal{A}_{\Lambda_{+}})\to S^{N}(\mathcal{A}_{\Lambda_{-}})
by
\Phi_{2}(a)=s
,
\Phi_{2}(a_{1})=a_{1}-s^{-1}
,
\Phi_{2}(a_{2})=a_{2}-s^{-1}
, and
\Phi_{2}
is the identity on all other generators of
S^{N}(\mathcal{A}_{\Lambda_{+}})
. Note that by Lemma
A.7
,
\Phi_{1}
and
\Phi_{2}
are both chain maps. By the above discussion, we conclude that the following diagram commutes:

\textstyle{S^{N}(\mathcal{A}_{\Lambda_{+}})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\Phi_{2}}
\textstyle{\mathcal{A}_{\Lambda_{+}^{\prime}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\Psi_{+}}
\scriptstyle{\cong}
\scriptstyle{\Phi_{1}}
\textstyle{S^{N}(\mathcal{A}_{\Lambda_{-}})}
\textstyle{\mathcal{A}_{\Lambda_{-}^{\prime}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces.}
\scriptstyle{\Psi_{-}}
\scriptstyle{\cong}

The cobordism map
\mathcal{A}_{\Lambda_{+}}\to\mathcal{A}_{\Lambda_{+}^{\prime}}
is simply the composition of the inclusion map
i:\thinspace\mathcal{A}_{\Lambda_{+}}\to S^{N}(\mathcal{A}_{\Lambda_{+}})
and the inverse of
\Psi_{+}
, and the cobordism map
\mathcal{A}_{\Lambda_{-}^{\prime}}\to\mathcal{A}_{\Lambda_{-}}
is the composition of
\Psi_{-}
and the projection map
p:\thinspace S^{N}(\mathcal{A}_{\Lambda_{-}})\to\mathcal{A}_{\Lambda_{-}}
.

We can now finally turn to the geometric cobordism map
\Phi_{L_{a}}:\thinspace\mathcal{A}_{\Lambda_{+}}\to\mathcal{A}_{\Lambda_{-}}
. To complete the proof of Proposition
A.1
, we want to show that
\Phi_{L_{a}}=\Omega\circ\Phi
for some link automorphism
\Omega
of
\Lambda_{-}
.

At this point we have broken down
\Phi_{L_{a}}
into a composition of three cobordism maps:
\Psi_{+}^{-1}\circ i:\thinspace\mathcal{A}_{\Lambda_{+}}\to\mathcal{A}_{\Lambda_{+}^{\prime}}
,
\Phi^{\prime}:\thinspace\mathcal{A}_{\Lambda_{+}^{\prime}}\to\mathcal{A}_{\Lambda_{-}^{\prime}}
, and
p\circ\Psi_{-}:\thinspace\mathcal{A}_{\Lambda_{-}^{\prime}}\to\mathcal{A}_{\Lambda_{-}}
. That is,
\Phi_{L_{a}}
is chain homotopy equivalent to the composition
p\circ\Psi_{-}\circ\Phi^{\prime}\circ\Psi_{+}^{-1}\circ i
of the five maps going around the sides of the following rectangle:

\textstyle{\mathcal{A}_{\Lambda_{+}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{i}
\scriptstyle{\Phi_{L_{a}}}
\textstyle{S^{N}(\mathcal{A}_{\Lambda_{+}})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\Phi_{2}}
\textstyle{\mathcal{A}_{\Lambda_{+}^{\prime}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\Psi_{+}}
\scriptstyle{\cong}
\scriptstyle{\Phi^{\prime}}
\textstyle{\mathcal{A}_{\Lambda_{-}}}
\textstyle{S^{N}(\mathcal{A}_{\Lambda_{-}})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{p}
\textstyle{\mathcal{A}_{\Lambda_{-}^{\prime}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces.}
\scriptstyle{\Psi_{-}}
\scriptstyle{\cong}

From Lemma
A.8
, there is a link automorphism
\Omega
of
\Lambda_{-}^{\prime}
such that
\Phi^{\prime}=\Omega\circ\Phi_{1}
. Since
\Lambda_{-}^{\prime}
and
\Lambda_{-}
are Legendrian isotopic,
\Omega
induces a link automorphism of
\Lambda_{-}
, which we also call
\Omega
, so that
\Omega
commutes with the chain map
p\circ\Psi_{-}:\thinspace\mathcal{A}_{\Lambda_{-}^{\prime}}\to\mathcal{A}_{\Lambda_{-}}
induced by the isotopy. Thus

\Phi_{L_{a}}\simeq p\circ\Psi_{-}\circ\Phi^{\prime}\circ\Psi_{+}^{-1}\circ i=p\circ\Psi_{-}\circ\Omega\circ\Phi_{1}\circ\Psi_{+}^{-1}\circ i=\Omega\circ p\circ\Psi_{-}\circ\Phi_{1}\circ\Psi_{+}^{-1}\circ i=\Omega\circ p\circ\Phi_{2}\circ i.

But
p\circ\Phi_{2}\circ i
is exactly equal to
\Phi
as defined in the statement of Proposition
A.1
, and we are done with the proof.

A.4.
Proof of Proposition
4.8

The remainder of this section is devoted to the proof of Proposition
4.8
. At this point, by Proposition
A.1
, we know the saddle cobordism map for a saddle flanked by mini-dips; to prove Proposition
4.8
, we just need to compose this map with maps corresponding to the Reidemeister II moves of adding and removing mini-dips. This is similar to the proof of Proposition
A.1
in the previous subsection, except that it will now be important to calculate these Reidemeister II maps in more detail.

Suppose that, as in the statement of Proposition
4.8
, we have a saddle cobordism between
\Lambda_{+}
and
\Lambda_{-}
, where the cobordism is given by resolving a proper contractible Reeb chord
a
of
\Lambda_{+}
. Let
\Lambda_{+}^{\prime}
be the result of adding a mini-dip to
\Lambda_{+}
just after
a
following the orientation of
\Lambda_{+}
, and let
\Lambda_{+}^{\prime\prime}
be result of further adding a mini-dip to
\Lambda_{+}^{\prime}
on the other side of
a
. Similarly define
\Lambda_{-}^{\prime}
and
\Lambda_{-}^{\prime\prime}
. Then
\Lambda_{\pm}^{\prime}
are obtained from
\Lambda_{\pm}
by a single Reidemeister II move,
\Lambda_{\pm}^{\prime\prime}
are obtained from
\Lambda_{\pm}^{\prime}
by another Reidemeister II move, and
\Lambda_{+}^{\prime\prime}
and
\Lambda_{-}^{\prime\prime}
are related by a saddle move of the precise form that we considered in Proposition
A.1
. See Figure
47
.

Figure 47.
Adding mini-dips to
\Lambda_{\pm}
.

The properness condition for
a
translates into the following result.

Lemma A.9
.

Given that the Reeb chord
a
is proper:

•

if
a_{q}
is any Reeb chord of
\Lambda_{+}^{\prime}
, and
\partial^{\prime}
denotes the differential on
\Lambda_{+}^{\prime}
, then any term in
\partial^{\prime}(a_{q})
that contains
a_{3}
must contain
a_{3}
exactly once and cannot contain
a_{1}
;

•

if
a_{q}
is any Reeb chord of
\Lambda_{+}^{\prime\prime}
, and
\partial^{\prime\prime}
denotes the differential on
\Lambda_{+}^{\prime\prime}
, then any term in
\partial^{\prime}(a_{q})
that contains
a_{4}
must contain
a_{4}
exactly once and cannot contain any of
a_{1},a_{2},a_{3}
.

Figure 48.
Turning an immersed disk
\Delta^{\prime}
for
\Lambda_{+}^{\prime}
into an immersed disk
\Delta
for
\Lambda_{+}
.

Proof.

We will establish the statement for
\Lambda_{+}^{\prime}
; the proof of the statement for
\Lambda_{+}^{\prime\prime}
is similar. If
a_{q}
is any of
a,a_{1},a_{3}
, then the statement is trivially true: by action considerations, the only term in
\partial^{\prime}(a_{q})
that could contain
a_{3}
is just the term
a_{3}
itself in
\partial^{\prime}(a_{1})
. Now assume
a_{q}
is not
a,a_{1},a_{3}
. Consider any word in
\partial^{\prime}(a_{q})
, corresponding to an immersed disk
\Delta^{\prime}
in
\Lambda_{+}^{\prime}
with sole
+
corner at
a_{q}
and a
-
corner at
a_{3}
. Then
\Delta^{\prime}
in turn produces an immersed disk
\Delta
in
\Lambda_{+}
, now possibly with concave corners at
a
: see Figure
48
. If
\Delta^{\prime}
contained multiple corners at
a_{3}
, or corners at both
a_{1}
and
a_{3}
, then the boundary of
\Delta
would pass through
\Pi_{xy}(a)
more than once, violating the properness condition from Definition
4.3
.
∎

We will now piece together the five maps
\mathcal{A}_{\Lambda_{+}}\to\mathcal{A}_{\Lambda_{+}^{\prime}}\to\mathcal{A}_{\Lambda_{+}^{\prime\prime}}\to\mathcal{A}_{\Lambda_{-}^{\prime\prime}}\to\mathcal{A}_{\Lambda_{-}^{\prime}}\to\mathcal{A}_{\Lambda_{-}}
to get the desired cobordism map. The central map
\mathcal{A}_{\Lambda_{+}^{\prime\prime}}\to\mathcal{A}_{\Lambda_{-}^{\prime\prime}}
has already been computed, while the remaining maps come from Reidemeister II isotopies.

We will focus for now on the map
\mathcal{A}_{\Lambda_{+}}\to\mathcal{A}_{\Lambda_{+}^{\prime}}
, which we call
\Psi_{+}^{\rightarrow}
. This is the chain map induced by adding a Legendrian Reidemeister II move, as derived in
[
Che02
,
ENS02
]
and summarized in Section
4.1
above, and we describe it explicitly now. Label the Reeb chords of
\Lambda_{+}
besides
a
as
a_{5},\ldots,a_{r}
, so that we can write
\mathcal{A}_{\Lambda_{+}}=\mathcal{A}(a,a_{5},\ldots,a_{r})
and
\mathcal{A}_{\Lambda_{+}^{\prime}}=\mathcal{A}(a,a_{1},a_{3},a_{5},\ldots,a_{r})
. We stabilize
\mathcal{A}_{\Lambda_{+}}
by adding two new generators
e_{1},e_{2}
with
|e_{1}|=0
,
|e_{2}|=-1
,
\partial(e_{1})=e_{2}
,
\partial(e_{2})=0
, to produce a new DGA
S(\mathcal{A}_{\Lambda_{+}})=\mathcal{A}(a,a_{5},\ldots,a_{r},e_{1},e_{2})
. As described in Section
4.1
and specifically defined in (
4.1
), there is a chain isomorphism
\Psi:\thinspace\mathcal{A}_{\Lambda_{+}^{\prime}}\to S(\mathcal{A}_{\Lambda_{+}})
, which in our case is defined by
\Psi(a_{1})=e_{1}
,
\Psi(a_{3})=-e_{2}
,
\Psi(a)=a
, and for
\ell\geq 5
,

\Psi(a_{\ell})=a_{\ell}-H\Psi\partial^{\prime}a_{\ell}

where
\partial^{\prime}
is the differential on
\Lambda_{+}^{\prime}
. Then
\Psi_{+}^{\rightarrow}
is defined to be equal to
\Psi^{-1}\circ i
.

We now claim that
\Psi_{+}^{\rightarrow}
satisfies the following formula, which can be compared to the definition of
\Phi^{\rightarrow}
from Section
4.2
.

Lemma A.10
.

For all
\ell\geq 5
, we have

(A.1)

\Psi_{+}^{\rightarrow}(a_{\ell})=a_{\ell}-\sum_{\Delta\in\Delta_{a}^{\rightarrow}(a_{\ell})}(-1)^{|w_{1}(\Delta)|}\operatorname{sgn}(\Delta)\Psi_{+}^{\rightarrow}(w_{1}(\Delta))a_{1}w_{2}(\Delta).

Proof.

Assume without loss of generality that
a,a_{5},\ldots,a_{r}
are ordered by height (note that
a
is contractible and thus has the shortest height). We first claim that for all
q\geq 5
,
\Psi(a_{q})-a_{q}
only includes terms that involve at least one
e_{1}
and no
e_{2}
: we abbreviate this condition by
\Psi(a_{q})-a_{q}=O(e_{1})
. We prove this by induction on
q
, where the base case is actually
a_{q}=a
(note
\Psi(a)-a=0
). For the induction step, note that
\Psi(a_{q})-a_{q}=-H\Psi\partial^{\prime}a_{q}
, and the right hand side only contains terms involving at least one
e_{1}
; we need to show that
H\Psi\partial^{\prime}a_{q}
does not involve
e_{2}
.

Consider any word
w
in
\partial^{\prime}a_{q}
. If
a_{3}
does not appear in
w
, then
w
involves only
a,a_{1},a_{5},\ldots,a_{q-1}
, and so by induction
\Psi(w)-w=O(e_{1})
and
H\Psi(w)=H(w)=0
. On the other hand, if
w
does involve
a_{3}
, then by Lemma
A.9
,
w=w_{1}a_{3}w_{2}
where
w_{1},w_{2}
involve only
a,a_{5},\ldots,a_{q-1}
; then by induction again,
H\Psi(w)=-H((\Psi(w_{1}))e_{2}(\Psi(w_{2})))=-H(w_{1}e_{2}\Psi(w_{2}))=\pm w_{1}e_{1}\Psi(w_{2})
does not involve
e_{2}
. This completes the proof that
\Psi(a_{q})-a_{q}=O(e_{1})
for all
q\geq 5
.

We now prove the lemma, again by induction on
\ell
. The base case is actually
\Psi_{+}^{\rightarrow}(a)=a
, which is (
A.1
) with
a=a_{\ell}
. For the induction step, we compute that:

\Psi_{+}^{\rightarrow}(a_{\ell})=\Psi^{-1}(a_{\ell})=a_{\ell}+\Psi^{-1}H\Psi\partial^{\prime}a_{\ell}.

Now suppose that
w
is a word in
\partial^{\prime}a_{\ell}
, and again apply Lemma
A.9
. If
w
does not contain
a_{3}
, then
H\Psi(w)=0
. If
w
does contain
a_{3}
, then we write
w=w_{1}a_{3}w_{2}
and compute:

H\Psi(w)=H\Psi(w_{1}a_{3}w_{2})=-H(\Psi(w_{1})e_{2}\Psi(w_{2}))=-H(w_{1}e_{2}\Psi(w_{2}))=(-1)^{|w_{1}|}w_{1}e_{1}\Psi(w_{2})

and thus

\Psi^{-1}H\Psi(w)=(-1)^{|w_{1}|}\Psi^{-1}(w_{1}e_{1}\Psi(w_{2}))=(-1)^{|w_{1}|}\Psi^{-1}(w_{1})a_{1}w_{2}=(-1)^{|w_{1}|}\Psi_{+}^{\rightarrow}(w_{1})a_{1}w_{2},

where we have used the fact that
w_{1}
does not involve
a_{1}
or
a_{3}
and thus
\Psi^{-1}(w_{1})=\Psi^{-1}i(w_{1})=\Psi_{+}^{\rightarrow}(w_{1})
. Finally note that the disk for
w
in
\Lambda_{+}^{\prime}
precisely corresponds to a disk
\Delta
in
\Delta_{a}^{\rightarrow}(a_{\ell})
in
\Lambda_{+}
, and that the sign for
w
in
\partial^{\prime}a_{\ell}
is
-\operatorname{sgn}(\Delta)
since
\Delta
replaces a corner at
a_{3}
with positive orientation sign with a corner at
a
with negative orientation sign. Now the signed sum of
\Psi^{-1}H\Psi(w)=\Psi_{+}^{\rightarrow}(w_{1})a_{1}w_{2}
over all disks in
\Delta_{a}^{\rightarrow}(a_{\ell})
gives (
A.1
), and this completes the induction.
∎

In a similar way, we write
\Psi_{+}^{\leftarrow}
for the cobordism map from

\mathcal{A}_{\Lambda_{+}^{\prime}}=\mathcal{A}(a,a_{1},a_{3},a_{5},\ldots,a_{r})
to
\mathcal{A}_{\Lambda_{+}^{\prime\prime}}=\mathcal{A}(a,a_{1},a_{2},a_{3},a_{4},a_{5},\ldots,a_{r})
induced by the Reidemeister II isotopy between
\Lambda_{+}^{\prime}
and
\Lambda_{+}^{\prime\prime}
.

Lemma A.11
.

For all
\ell\geq 5
,

we have

\Psi_{+}^{\leftarrow}(a_{\ell})=a_{\ell}-\sum_{\Delta\in\Delta_{a}^{\leftarrow}(a_{\ell})}(-1)^{|w_{1}(\Delta)|}\operatorname{sgn}(\Delta)\Psi_{+}^{\rightarrow}(w_{1}(\Delta))a_{2}w_{2}(\Delta).

Proof.

This is essentially identical to the proof of Lemma
A.1
. Given our choice of orientation signs, there are two sign differences here from the proof of Lemma
A.1
:
\Psi(a_{4})
is now
e_{2}
rather than
-e_{2}
, and the sign of a word contributing to
\partial^{\prime}(a_{\ell})
is now equal to
+\operatorname{sgn}(\Delta)
rather than
-\operatorname{sgn}(\Delta)
for the corresponding disk
\Delta\in\Delta_{a}^{\leftarrow}(a_{\ell})
. These two sign changes cancel out. One other subtle difference is that if we follow the proof of the previous lemma, then
\Delta_{a}^{\leftarrow}(a_{\ell})
in the statement of the present lemma should be for
\Lambda_{+}^{\prime}
rather than for
\Lambda_{+}
. However, by the properness condition for
a
, there is a one-to-one correspondence between disks in
\Delta_{a}^{\leftarrow}(a_{\ell})
for
\Lambda_{+}^{\prime}
and
\Lambda_{+}
, and so the desired formula holds for either form of
\Delta_{a}^{\leftarrow}(a_{\ell})
.
∎

We can now finally piece together our various subsidiary results to prove Proposition
4.8
. To distinguish between the saddle cobordisms in the dipped and undipped settings, let
L_{a}
be the cobordism between
\Lambda_{+}
and
\Lambda_{-}
as in the statement of Proposition
4.8
, and let
\widetilde{L}_{a}
be the cobordism between
\Lambda_{+}^{\prime\prime}
and
\Lambda_{-}^{\prime\prime}
. As shown in Figure
47
, we can concatenate
\widetilde{L}_{a}
and four Lagrangians coming from Legendrian isotopies to create a five-story cobordism between
\Lambda_{+}
and
\Lambda_{-}
which is Hamiltonian isotopic to
L_{a}
: from top to bottom, the five cobordisms go between
\Lambda_{+}
,
\Lambda_{+}^{\prime}
,
\Lambda_{+}^{\prime\prime}
,
\Lambda_{-}^{\prime\prime}
,
\Lambda_{-}^{\prime}
, and
\Lambda_{-}
.

The chain map
\Phi_{L_{a}}:\thinspace\mathcal{A}_{\Lambda_{+}}\to\mathcal{A}_{\Lambda_{-}}
is then chain homotopic to the composition of the chain maps coming from the five cobordisms. We summarize this in the following diagram, which commutes up to chain homotopy:

\textstyle{\mathcal{A}_{\Lambda_{+}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\Psi_{+}^{\rightarrow}}
\scriptstyle{\Phi_{L_{a}}}
\textstyle{\mathcal{A}_{\Lambda_{+}^{\prime}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\Psi_{+}^{\leftarrow}}
\textstyle{\mathcal{A}_{\Lambda_{+}^{\prime\prime}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\Phi_{\widetilde{L}_{a}}}
\textstyle{\mathcal{A}_{\Lambda_{-}}}
\textstyle{\mathcal{A}_{\Lambda_{-}^{\prime}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{p_{2}}
\textstyle{\mathcal{A}_{\Lambda_{-}^{\prime\prime}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces.}
\scriptstyle{p_{1}}

Here
\Psi_{+}^{\rightarrow}
and
\Psi_{+}^{\leftarrow}
are the maps computed in Lemmas
A.1
and
A.11
, while
p_{1}
and
p_{2}
are the maps induced by the reverse Reidemeister II moves from
\Lambda_{-}^{\prime\prime}
to
\Lambda_{-}^{\prime}
and from
\Lambda_{-}^{\prime}
to
\Lambda_{-}
. By Remark
4.2
, these last two maps (which correspond to
p\circ\Psi
in Remark
4.2
) are given simply by projection:
p_{1}(a_{2})=p_{1}(a_{4})=p_{2}(a_{1})=p_{2}(a_{3})=0
and
p_{1},p_{2}
are the identity on all other generators.

Now by Proposition
A.1
,
\Phi_{\widetilde{L}_{a}}=\Omega\circ\Phi
where
\Omega
is a link automorphism of
\Lambda_{-}^{\prime\prime}
and
\Phi
is the map given in the statement of the proposition. Since
\Lambda_{-}^{\prime\prime}
and
\Lambda_{-}
are isotopic,
\Omega
induces a link automorphism of
\Lambda_{-}
which we also denote by
\Omega
, and
p_{2}\circ p_{1}\circ\Omega=\Omega\circ p_{2}\circ p_{1}
. At this point we have:

\Phi_{L_{a}}\simeq p_{2}\circ p_{1}\circ\Phi_{\widetilde{L}_{a}}\circ\Psi_{+}^{\leftarrow}\circ\Psi_{+}^{\rightarrow}=\Omega\circ p_{2}\circ p_{1}\circ\Phi\circ\Psi_{+}^{\leftarrow}\circ\Psi_{+}^{\rightarrow}.

We will be done if we can show that the composition
p_{2}\circ p_{1}\circ\Phi\circ\Psi_{+}^{\leftarrow}\circ\Psi_{+}^{\rightarrow}
is equal to the map
\Phi_{L_{a}}^{\operatorname{comb}}=\Phi^{\leftarrow}\circ\Phi^{\rightarrow}\circ\Phi_{0}
from Proposition
4.8
. But
\Phi_{L_{a}}^{\operatorname{comb}}
is specifically designed so that this is the case. Specifically, if
a_{\ell}
is any Reeb chord of
\Lambda_{+}
besides
a
, then
\Phi^{\rightarrow}(a_{\ell})
and
\Phi^{\leftarrow}(a_{\ell})
are precisely the result of replacing
a_{1}
and
a_{2}
by
-s^{-1}
in the expressions for
\Psi_{+}^{\rightarrow}(a_{\ell})
and
\Psi_{+}^{\leftarrow}(a_{\ell})
from Lemmas
A.1
and
A.11
. But by the definition of
\Phi
, this replacement is exactly the effect of composing with the map
p_{2}\circ p_{1}\circ\Phi
, which sends
a_{1},a_{2}
to
-s^{-1}
and sends
a_{\ell}
to itself for
\ell\geq 5
. It follows that

\Phi_{L_{a}}^{\operatorname{comb}}(a_{\ell})=(\Phi^{\leftarrow}\circ\Phi^{\rightarrow})(a_{\ell})=(p_{2}\circ p_{1}\circ\Phi)((\Psi_{+}^{\leftarrow}\circ\Psi_{+}^{\rightarrow})(a_{\ell})

for all
\ell
. Combined with the fact that
\Phi_{L_{a}}^{\operatorname{comb}}(a)=s=(p_{2}\circ p_{1}\circ\Phi\circ\Psi_{+}^{\leftarrow}\circ\Psi_{+}^{\rightarrow})(a)
, this establishes that

\Phi_{L_{a}}^{\operatorname{comb}}=p_{2}\circ p_{1}\circ\Phi\circ\Psi_{+}^{\leftarrow}\circ\Psi_{+}^{\rightarrow}
. The proof of Proposition
4.8
is complete.
